========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I, uh, I appreciate you seeing me. I’m not entirely sure where to begin, honestly, because it’s a lot to unpack, and it’s been going on for a while now. I work as a personal financial advisor, you see, mostly dealing with clients in the arts and finance sectors here in Cincinnati. It’s a tight-knit community, very interconnected, lots of networking events, gallery openings, charity dinners, that sort of thing. But lately, well, for a good stretch of time now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain individuals in these circles are... tracking me. Not in an overt way, but quietly monitoring exactly which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m bringing on board, my entire portfolio. And it’s not just a passing thought; they’re sending me these little signals to let me know they’re watching. Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, "Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town," but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. 

Like, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.

Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.

Not in an overt way, but quietly monitoring exactly which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m bringing on board, my entire portfolio. And it’s not just a passing thought; they’re sending me these little signals to let me know they’re watching. Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I mean, sure, I'm talking a lot right now, but that's just because I'm trying to explain all this to you so you understand exactly what's happening. But in my day-to-day life, I'm pretty measured. I have to be. You can't be bouncing off the walls when you're advising someone on a million-dollar trust fund. So, no, I haven't had any big swings like that, high or low. Well, see, that’s exactly why I came in to talk to someone in the first place. Because the whole "turned out not to be true" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting. To me, it feels incredibly, undeniably real. 

For a while now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain people—specifically this sort of overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati—are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And they aren't just tracking it, they’re going out of their way to send me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It plays out in these hyper-specific ways, you know? Like through the invitations I receive in the mail, or the specific panel topics at these networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at a charity dinner. I’ll sit down at a table of ten, and the two people placed immediately to my left and right will be directly connected to a private trust I was just reviewing in my office that very morning. 

And, you know, people will tell me I'm overthinking it. A colleague or a friend will say, "Oh, the event planners just seat people by donor bracket, it's totally random, look at the spreadsheet." And I hear them, I really do. I try to be a logical person—I have to be, with my degree and my job. I sit there and I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that maybe the panel topic was planned six months ago and it's just a fluke that it perfectly mirrors my current client roster. But even when they show me that supposed evidence, or explain the logistics of the event, I just... I can’t really let it go. I can't shake the absolute certainty that it's intentional, that they are watching my moves. Because how many coincidences can you have before it's a deliberate pattern, right? So I guess that’s the thing I'm so sure about, even when everyone else is trying to prove to me that it's just in my head.

Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I haven't lost interest in my work—in fact, I'm probably more focused on it now than ever, given everything that's going on with the networking events and trying to figure out who's connected to who. I still enjoy a good cup of coffee in the morning, I still like reading the financial times... I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just stressful, you know? But stress isn't the same as being depressed. \n\nAnd as for the other thing, feeling unusually energized or not needing sleep... no, definitely not. If anything, I'm exhausted. Like I said before, my brain is just constantly going, trying to decode all these interactions, so when I finally do get to bed, I'm drained. I definitely need my sleep. I'm not staying up all night painting or coming up with wild business schemes or talking a mile a minute. I mean, sure, I'm talking a lot right now, but that's just because I'm trying to explain all this to you so you understand exactly what's happening. But in my day-to-day life, I'm pretty measured. I have to be. You can't be bouncing off the walls when you're advising someone on a million-dollar trust fund. So, no, I haven't had any big swings like that, high or low. It's just this constant, steady pressure of dealing with the signals and the subtext, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you were absolutely sure about something that turned out not to be true, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s exactly why I came in to talk to someone in the first place. Because the whole \"turned out not to be true\" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting. To me, it feels incredibly, undeniably real. \n\nFor a while now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain people—specifically this sort of overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati—are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And they aren't just tracking it, they’re going out of their way to send me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It plays out in these hyper-specific ways, you know? Like through the invitations I receive in the mail, or the specific panel topics at these networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at a charity dinner. I’ll sit down at a table of ten, and the two people placed immediately to my left and right will be directly connected to a private trust I was just reviewing in my office that very morning. \n\nAnd, you know, people will tell me I'm overthinking it. A colleague or a friend will say, \"Oh, the event planners just seat people by donor bracket, it's totally random, look at the spreadsheet.\" And I hear them, I really do. I try to be a logical person—I have to be, with my degree and my job. I sit there and I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that maybe the panel topic was planned six months ago and it's just a fluke that it perfectly mirrors my current client roster. But even when they show me that supposed evidence, or explain the logistics of the event, I just... I can’t really let it go. I can't shake the absolute certainty that it's intentional, that they are watching my moves. Because how many coincidences can you have before it's a deliberate pattern, right? So I guess that’s the thing I'm so sure about, even when everyone else is trying to prove to me that it's just in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I haven't lost interest in my work—in fact, I'm probably more focused on it now than ever, given everything that's going on with the networking events and trying to figure out who's connected to who. I still enjoy a good cup of coffee in the morning, I still like reading the financial times... I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just stressful, you know? But stress isn't the same as being depressed. \n\nAnd as for the other thing, feeling unusually energized or not needing sleep... no, definitely not. If anything, I'm exhausted. Like I said before, my brain is just constantly going, trying to decode all these interactions, so when I finally do get to bed, I'm drained. I definitely need my sleep. I'm not staying up all night painting or coming up with wild business schemes or talking a mile a minute. I mean, sure, I'm talking a lot right now, but that's just because I'm trying to explain all this to you so you understand exactly what's happening. But in my day-to-day life, I'm pretty measured. I have to be. You can't be bouncing off the walls when you're advising someone on a million-dollar trust fund. So, no, I haven't had any big swings like that, high or low. It's just this constant, steady pressure of dealing with the signals and the subtext, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you were absolutely sure about something that turned out not to be true, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s exactly why I came in to talk to someone in the first place. Because the whole \"turned out not to be true\" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting. To me, it feels incredibly, undeniably real. \n\nFor a while now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain people—specifically this sort of overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati—are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And they aren't just tracking it, they’re going out of their way to send me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It plays out in these hyper-specific ways, you know? Like through the invitations I receive in the mail, or the specific panel topics at these networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at a charity dinner. I’ll sit down at a table of ten, and the two people placed immediately to my left and right will be directly connected to a private trust I was just reviewing in my office that very morning. \n\nAnd, you know, people will tell me I'm overthinking it. A colleague or a friend will say, \"Oh, the event planners just seat people by donor bracket, it's totally random, look at the spreadsheet.\" And I hear them, I really do. I try to be a logical person—I have to be, with my degree and my job. I sit there and I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that maybe the panel topic was planned six months ago and it's just a fluke that it perfectly mirrors my current client roster. But even when they show me that supposed evidence, or explain the logistics of the event, I just... I can’t really let it go. I can't shake the absolute certainty that it's intentional, that they are watching my moves. Because how many coincidences can you have before it's a deliberate pattern, right? So I guess that’s the thing I'm so sure about, even when everyone else is trying to prove to me that it's just in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen people have explained to you that the events you notice could be coincidences or planned in advance, do you accept that explanation, or do you still feel certain that the events are specifically directed at you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with and sending me little signals through invitations, panel topics, even who sits near me at events, and even when I tell myself it could just be coincidence, I can’t really let it go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, right, like I was just trying to explain a second ago, I really, really try to accept those explanations. I do. I sit there and I listen to my colleague tell me that a panel topic was booked six months in advance, or I look at the event planner's seating chart, and my logical brain—the part of me that got my bachelor's degree and analyzes complex financial data all day—tries to say, "Okay, yes, that makes sense on paper." But... no. Ultimately, no, I don't accept it. I can't. 

Because the moment I step back into that room, or the moment I open another invitation in the mail, I get this overwhelming, undeniable certainty that it is absolutely, specifically directed at me. The arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, they are incredibly tight-knit, right? Everyone knows everyone, money moves between the same few institutions and galleries. And I am just completely convinced, down to my bones, that these specific people in those circles are quietly tracking exactly which clients I'm meeting with behind closed doors. 

It's not just a passing thought that I can brush off. It's this heavy, constant awareness. I'll be sitting there at a charity dinner, looking at the two people seated next to me, realizing they tie perfectly back to a portfolio I was just reviewing, and I'll tell myself, "It's just a coincidence, it's just random chance." I repeat it in my head. But I can't let it go. I just can't. Because how can it be random when the signals are just so incredibly precise? The panel topics, the invitations, the seating... it all perfectly aligns with my private client meetings. So, even when people try to explain it away with logistics or spreadsheets, it doesn't change what I know I'm experiencing. I hear their explanations, but deep down, I am absolutely certain they are tracking me and sending me these little signals. I just wish I knew exactly what their end game was, you know? Because if I could just figure out the 'why', maybe I could finally get some peace of mind, but instead I'm just constantly analyzing every single interaction trying to stay one step ahead of it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, that is kind of exactly what I have been trying to explain to you, isn't it? I guess when you say strange or impossible, you are talking about the exact reason I came in here to see you in the first place. 

Because, yes, absolutely, other people think it is completely strange. When I try to explain this to anyone else—when I tell them that this specific, overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati is quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with behind closed doors—people look at me like I have completely lost my grip on reality. They think the logistics of it are impossible. They ask me how anyone could possibly know who I met with on a Tuesday morning and then manage to send me a signal about it by Thursday. They think the idea of them using these little signals, like the specific wording on charity invitations, or the exact topics they choose for financial panels, or exactly who gets seated next to me at these networking events... they think the idea of all that being deliberately coordinated is just totally outside the realm of the real world. They think nobody has the time or the resources to pull off something like that just to mess with a personal financial advisor.

And honestly, I hear them. I really do. Like I said before, I try to be a logical person. If someone else sat down and told me this exact same story, I might think it sounded like a spy novel. I might think it was impossible, too. But the thing is, I am the one experiencing it. When you are the one sitting at the table, and you realize the two people placed next to you are tied perfectly back to a confidential trust fund you were just reviewing in your office a few hours ago... well, it does not feel impossible anymore. It feels incredibly, terrifyingly real. 

So, yes, other people definitely consider it strange. And even when I sit there and force my logical brain to take over, even when I tell myself that maybe everyone else is right and it could just be one massive, unbelievable string of coincidences, I just cannot let it go. I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. So I do not know if that answers your question about things being outside the realm of what people expect, but that is the reality I am dealing with every single day, regardless of how impossible anyone else thinks it is.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F43.22, F44.89\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I, uh, I appreciate you seeing me. I’m not entirely sure where to begin, honestly, because it’s a lot to unpack, and it’s been going on for a while now. I work as a personal financial advisor, you see, mostly dealing with clients in the arts and finance sectors here in Cincinnati. It’s a tight-knit community, very interconnected, lots of networking events, gallery openings, charity dinners, that sort of thing. But lately, well, for a good stretch of time now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain individuals in these circles are... tracking me. Not in an overt way, but quietly monitoring exactly which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m bringing on board, my entire portfolio. And it’s not just a passing thought; they’re sending me these little signals to let me know they’re watching. Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I haven't lost interest in my work—in fact, I'm probably more focused on it now than ever, given everything that's going on with the networking events and trying to figure out who's connected to who. I still enjoy a good cup of coffee in the morning, I still like reading the financial times... I'm not sitting around crying or feeling like life isn't worth living. It's just stressful, you know? But stress isn't the same as being depressed. \n\nAnd as for the other thing, feeling unusually energized or not needing sleep... no, definitely not. If anything, I'm exhausted. Like I said before, my brain is just constantly going, trying to decode all these interactions, so when I finally do get to bed, I'm drained. I definitely need my sleep. I'm not staying up all night painting or coming up with wild business schemes or talking a mile a minute. I mean, sure, I'm talking a lot right now, but that's just because I'm trying to explain all this to you so you understand exactly what's happening. But in my day-to-day life, I'm pretty measured. I have to be. You can't be bouncing off the walls when you're advising someone on a million-dollar trust fund. So, no, I haven't had any big swings like that, high or low. It's just this constant, steady pressure of dealing with the signals and the subtext, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you were absolutely sure about something that turned out not to be true, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s exactly why I came in to talk to someone in the first place. Because the whole \"turned out not to be true\" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting. To me, it feels incredibly, undeniably real. \n\nFor a while now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain people—specifically this sort of overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati—are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And they aren't just tracking it, they’re going out of their way to send me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It plays out in these hyper-specific ways, you know? Like through the invitations I receive in the mail, or the specific panel topics at these networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at a charity dinner. I’ll sit down at a table of ten, and the two people placed immediately to my left and right will be directly connected to a private trust I was just reviewing in my office that very morning. \n\nAnd, you know, people will tell me I'm overthinking it. A colleague or a friend will say, \"Oh, the event planners just seat people by donor bracket, it's totally random, look at the spreadsheet.\" And I hear them, I really do. I try to be a logical person—I have to be, with my degree and my job. I sit there and I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that maybe the panel topic was planned six months ago and it's just a fluke that it perfectly mirrors my current client roster. But even when they show me that supposed evidence, or explain the logistics of the event, I just... I can’t really let it go. I can't shake the absolute certainty that it's intentional, that they are watching my moves. Because how many coincidences can you have before it's a deliberate pattern, right? So I guess that’s the thing I'm so sure about, even when everyone else is trying to prove to me that it's just in my head.\n\nDoctor: When people have explained to you that the events you notice could be coincidences or planned in advance, do you accept that explanation, or do you still feel certain that the events are specifically directed at you?\n\nPatient: Well, right, like I was just trying to explain a second ago, I really, really try to accept those explanations. I do. I sit there and I listen to my colleague tell me that a panel topic was booked six months in advance, or I look at the event planner's seating chart, and my logical brain—the part of me that got my bachelor's degree and analyzes complex financial data all day—tries to say, \"Okay, yes, that makes sense on paper.\" But... no. Ultimately, no, I don't accept it. I can't. \n\nBecause the moment I step back into that room, or the moment I open another invitation in the mail, I get this overwhelming, undeniable certainty that it is absolutely, specifically directed at me. The arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, they are incredibly tight-knit, right? Everyone knows everyone, money moves between the same few institutions and galleries. And I am just completely convinced, down to my bones, that these specific people in those circles are quietly tracking exactly which clients I'm meeting with behind closed doors. \n\nIt's not just a passing thought that I can brush off. It's this heavy, constant awareness. I'll be sitting there at a charity dinner, looking at the two people seated next to me, realizing they tie perfectly back to a portfolio I was just reviewing, and I'll tell myself, \"It's just a coincidence, it's just random chance.\" I repeat it in my head. But I can't let it go. I just can't. Because how can it be random when the signals are just so incredibly precise? The panel topics, the invitations, the seating... it all perfectly aligns with my private client meetings. So, even when people try to explain it away with logistics or spreadsheets, it doesn't change what I know I'm experiencing. I hear their explanations, but deep down, I am absolutely certain they are tracking me and sending me these little signals. I just wish I knew exactly what their end game was, you know? Because if I could just figure out the 'why', maybe I could finally get some peace of mind, but instead I'm just constantly analyzing every single interaction trying to stay one step ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that other people would consider strange or impossible, like thoughts that are completely outside the realm of what most people would normally expect to happen in the real world?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, that is kind of exactly what I have been trying to explain to you, isn't it? I guess when you say strange or impossible, you are talking about the exact reason I came in here to see you in the first place. \n\nBecause, yes, absolutely, other people think it is completely strange. When I try to explain this to anyone else—when I tell them that this specific, overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati is quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with behind closed doors—people look at me like I have completely lost my grip on reality. They think the logistics of it are impossible. They ask me how anyone could possibly know who I met with on a Tuesday morning and then manage to send me a signal about it by Thursday. They think the idea of them using these little signals, like the specific wording on charity invitations, or the exact topics they choose for financial panels, or exactly who gets seated next to me at these networking events... they think the idea of all that being deliberately coordinated is just totally outside the realm of the real world. They think nobody has the time or the resources to pull off something like that just to mess with a personal financial advisor.\n\nAnd honestly, I hear them. I really do. Like I said before, I try to be a logical person. If someone else sat down and told me this exact same story, I might think it sounded like a spy novel. I might think it was impossible, too. But the thing is, I am the one experiencing it. When you are the one sitting at the table, and you realize the two people placed next to you are tied perfectly back to a confidential trust fund you were just reviewing in your office a few hours ago... well, it does not feel impossible anymore. It feels incredibly, terrifyingly real. \n\nSo, yes, other people definitely consider it strange. And even when I sit there and force my logical brain to take over, even when I tell myself that maybe everyone else is right and it could just be one massive, unbelievable string of coincidences, I just cannot let it go. I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. So I do not know if that answers your question about things being outside the realm of what people expect, but that is the reality I am dealing with every single day, regardless of how impossible anyone else thinks it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I sit there and I listen to my colleague tell me that a panel topic was booked six months in advance, or I look at the event planner's seating chart, and my logical brain—the part of me that got my bachelor's degree and analyzes complex financial data all day—tries to say, \"Okay, yes, that makes sense on paper.\" But... no. Ultimately, no, I don't accept it. I can't. \n\nBecause the moment I step back into that room, or the moment I open another invitation in the mail, I get this overwhelming, undeniable certainty that it is absolutely, specifically directed at me. The arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, they are incredibly tight-knit, right? Everyone knows everyone, money moves between the same few institutions and galleries. It's this heavy, constant awareness. They think the logistics of it are impossible. They ask me how anyone could possibly know who I met with on a Tuesday morning and then manage to send me a signal about it by Thursday. They think the idea of them using these little signals, like the specific wording on charity invitations, or the exact topics they choose for financial panels, or exactly who gets seated next to me at these networking events... they think the idea of all that being deliberately coordinated is just totally outside the realm of the real world. They think nobody has the time or the resources to pull off something like that just to mess with a personal financial advisor.\n\nAnd honestly, I hear them. I really do. Like I said before, I try to be a logical person. If someone else sat down and told me this exact same story, I might think it sounded like a spy novel. I might think it was impossible, too. But the thing is, I am the one experiencing it. When you are the one sitting at the table, and you realize the two people placed next to you are tied perfectly back to a confidential trust fund you were just reviewing in your office a few hours ago... well, it does not feel impossible anymore. It feels incredibly, terrifyingly real. \n\nSo, yes, other people definitely consider it strange. I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. I feel like we are going in circles a little bit here, doctor, because... well, honestly, no. No, it hasn't changed. Like I have been trying to explain since I first sat down in that chair, the whole reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know I keep bringing it back to that, but it is just so consuming. 

When someone puts what you're calling "clear evidence" right in front of my face—like, say, an email chain from an event coordinator showing how they randomized a guest list—maybe for a fraction of a second, my logical brain tries to grab onto it. I will literally stand in my kitchen, holding an invitation or a seating chart, and I will force myself to say out loud, "This is just a coincidence. They didn't intend this for you." I really, truly try to talk myself down. But the relief, if you can even call it that, doesn't last. It never lasts. 

Because then I start thinking about it, and I realize, well, of course they have an email chain that makes it look random. They are smart people, they aren't going to leave an obvious paper trail, right? And then the very next week, they are sending me little signals again through new invitations, or the specific panel topics at a symposium, or, again, exactly who sits near me at these events. It just keeps happening. So even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, and even when I look right at the supposed proof that it has nothing to do with me... I can’t really let it go. The underlying certainty never actually shifts. It is just this constant, heavy weight, and no amount of evidence they show me seems to actually turn it off.

It feels incredibly, terrifyingly real. \n\nSo, yes, other people definitely consider it strange. And even when I sit there and force my logical brain to take over, even when I tell myself that maybe everyone else is right and it could just be one massive, unbelievable string of coincidences, I just cannot let it go. I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. So I do not know if that answers your question about things being outside the realm of what people expect, but that is the reality I am dealing with every single day, regardless of how impossible anyone else thinks it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your belief that these signals are being sent to you has ever changed, even slightly, when faced with clear evidence that they were not intended for you?\n\nPatient: I feel like we are going in circles a little bit here, doctor, because... well, honestly, no. No, it hasn't changed. Like I have been trying to explain since I first sat down in that chair, the whole reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know I keep bringing it back to that, but it is just so consuming. \n\nWhen someone puts what you're calling \"clear evidence\" right in front of my face—like, say, an email chain from an event coordinator showing how they randomized a guest list—maybe for a fraction of a second, my logical brain tries to grab onto it. I will literally stand in my kitchen, holding an invitation or a seating chart, and I will force myself to say out loud, \"This is just a coincidence. They didn't intend this for you.\" I really, truly try to talk myself down. But the relief, if you can even call it that, doesn't last. It never lasts. \n\nBecause then I start thinking about it, and I realize, well, of course they have an email chain that makes it look random. They are smart people, they aren't going to leave an obvious paper trail, right? And then the very next week, they are sending me little signals again through new invitations, or the specific panel topics at a symposium, or, again, exactly who sits near me at these events. It just keeps happening. So even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, and even when I look right at the supposed proof that it has nothing to do with me... I can’t really let it go. The underlying certainty never actually shifts. It is just this constant, heavy weight, and no amount of evidence they show me seems to actually turn it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Look, doctor, I know we keep circling back to this same exact point, and I really don't mean to sound like a broken record, but I just... I need you to grasp the sheer scope of this, because it is exhausting. Like I've been saying, the entire reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. 

And when you ask about being presented with "undeniable proof" and how long it takes for me to feel certain again... it's practically instantaneous. It doesn't go away. Let me give you a very specific example so you understand what I'm dealing with. Just last month, I actually went up to one of the gala coordinators at a museum fundraiser. I was so worked up, I just casually asked her how they decided the seating for the evening, because I was placed right next to a prominent gallery director whose private trust accounts I had just finished auditing that very morning. 

And she literally pulled up her iPad right there in the foyer and showed me the event management software. She showed me how it auto-generates the seating arrangements based entirely on RSVP timestamps and whether people requested the vegetarian meal. It was right there in black and white. Data. Algorithms. And for a second—literally just a fraction of a second—looking at that screen, I told myself, 'See? It's just a computer program. It's random.' I really tried to lean into that.

But... how long did it take for the certainty to come back? Honestly, by the time I walked away from her and reached the coat check, the feeling was already rushing back over me. Maybe two minutes? Three at the most? Because as I was walking, I started thinking, well, who programmed the software? Who inputted the parameters? Who has access to the backend of the system? They are constantly sending me little signals through the invitations, the panel topics at our quarterly symposiums, even who sits near me at these events... and they are absolutely smart enough to make it look like a randomized computer output if anyone ever asks. 

So, no matter what spreadsheet or software they show me, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The "proof" you're talking about just ends up feeling like another layer of the whole operation. I just can't shake it, no matter how hard I try to rationalize it away.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean... fully accept? Like, one hundred percent, without a single shadow of a doubt, just walk away and never think about it again? No. Honestly, doctor, no. 

I know we are kind of beating a dead horse here, and I know I keep circling back to the exact same explanation, but you have to understand that this is my entire life right now. It is exhausting to live with. I came in to see you specifically because, for a while now, I have been utterly convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And I know I keep saying that, but I need you to understand how all-encompassing it is when you are the one living through it. 

When they send me these little signals—whether it is the specific phrasing on the charity invitations they mail to my office, or the highly specific macroeconomic topics they suddenly decide to feature on a panel discussion, or, you know, exactly who ends up in the chair next to me at a networking dinner—I just can't turn a blind eye to it. I really try. I will sit in my car after one of these events, just gripping the steering wheel in the parking garage, and I will literally have a debate out loud with myself. I will say, 'Look, you are a rational guy, you work with numbers and portfolios, this could all just be one giant, ridiculous coincidence.' I try so hard to force myself to fully accept that it's random, just like you are asking me. I try to just breathe and let the logic take over.

But... no. I never fully accept it. Even when I sit there and tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The doubt always creeps right back in within minutes. It is like a splinter in my brain that I just can't reach. I start thinking about the statistical probability of all these things lining up perfectly with my confidential client list, and the rationalization just falls apart. So, I wish I could give you a nice, clean example of a time where I just brushed it off and moved on with my day, but that just hasn't happened. I just can't let it go.

I will say, 'Look, you are a rational guy, you work with numbers and portfolios, this could all just be one giant, ridiculous coincidence.' I start thinking about the statistical probability of all these things lining up perfectly with my confidential client list, and the rationalization just falls apart. So, I wish I could give you a nice, clean example of a time where I just brushed it off and moved on with my day, but that just hasn't happened. I just can't let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But the thing is, I'm just holding my breath. I haven't actually stopped believing it; I'm just suppressing it through sheer willpower for a minute or two. It never lasts. \n\nBecause then I start thinking about it, and I realize, well, of course they have an email chain that makes it look random. They are smart people, they aren't going to leave an obvious paper trail, right? And then the very next week, they are sending me little signals again through new invitations, or the specific panel topics at a symposium, or, again, exactly who sits near me at these events. It just keeps happening. So even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, and even when I look right at the supposed proof that it has nothing to do with me... I can’t really let it go. The underlying certainty never actually shifts. It is just this constant, heavy weight, and no amount of evidence they show me seems to actually turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were presented with clear, undeniable proof that these signals weren't actually directed at you, and if so, how long did it take before you started feeling certain again that they were?\n\nPatient: Look, doctor, I know we keep circling back to this same exact point, and I really don't mean to sound like a broken record, but I just... I need you to grasp the sheer scope of this, because it is exhausting. Like I've been saying, the entire reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. \n\nAnd when you ask about being presented with \"undeniable proof\" and how long it takes for me to feel certain again... it's practically instantaneous. She showed me how it auto-generates the seating arrangements based entirely on RSVP timestamps and whether people requested the vegetarian meal. It was right there in black and white. Data. Algorithms. And for a second—literally just a fraction of a second—looking at that screen, I told myself, 'See? It's just a computer program. It's random.' I really tried to lean into that.\n\nBut... how long did it take for the certainty to come back? Honestly, by the time I walked away from her and reached the coat check, the feeling was already rushing back over me. Maybe two minutes? Three at the most? Because as I was walking, I started thinking, well, who programmed the software? Who inputted the parameters? Who has access to the backend of the system? They are constantly sending me little signals through the invitations, the panel topics at our quarterly symposiums, even who sits near me at these events... and they are absolutely smart enough to make it look like a randomized computer output if anyone ever asks. \n\nSo, no matter what spreadsheet or software they show me, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. I try so hard to force myself to fully accept that it's random, just like you are asking me. I try to just breathe and let the logic take over.\n\nBut... no. I never fully accept it. A month? Well, I mean... obviously, before all this started, my entire life was a period where I didn't feel like this. The highly specific wording on the charity invitations, the way certain trust managers and gallery directors were suddenly coordinating their moves and panel topics around my confidential auditing schedule. It all just clicked into place about eight weeks ago. 

So, within that two-month window, there hasn't been any sort of break. Not a month, not a week, not even a quiet weekend. It’s been a constant, everyday reality for the last two months straight. I’m not entirely sure where to begin, honestly, because it’s a lot to unpack, and it’s been going on for a while now. I work as a personal financial advisor, you see, mostly dealing with clients in the arts and finance sectors here in Cincinnati. It’s a tight-knit community, very interconnected, lots of networking events, gallery openings, charity dinners, that sort of thing. But lately, well, for a good stretch of time now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain individuals in these circles are... tracking me. Not in an overt way, but quietly monitoring exactly which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m bringing on board, my entire portfolio. Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios.
