I hear their explanations, but deep down, I am absolutely certain they are tracking me and sending me these little signals. I just wish I knew exactly what their end game was, you know? Because if I could just figure out the 'why', maybe I could finally get some peace of mind, but instead I'm just constantly analyzing every single interaction trying to stay one step ahead of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that other people would consider strange or impossible, like thoughts that are completely outside the realm of what most people would normally expect to happen in the real world?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, that is kind of exactly what I have been trying to explain to you, isn't it? I guess when you say strange or impossible, you are talking about the exact reason I came in here to see you in the first place. \n\nBecause, yes, absolutely, other people think it is completely strange. When I try to explain this to anyone else—when I tell them that this specific, overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati is quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with behind closed doors—people look at me like I have completely lost my grip on reality. They think the logistics of it are impossible. They ask me how anyone could possibly know who I met with on a Tuesday morning and then manage to send me a signal about it by Thursday. They think the idea of them using these little signals, like the specific wording on charity invitations, or the exact topics they choose for financial panels, or exactly who gets seated next to me at these networking events... they think the idea of all that being deliberately coordinated is just totally outside the realm of the real world. They think nobody has the time or the resources to pull off something like that just to mess with a personal financial advisor.\n\nAnd honestly, I hear them. I really do. Like I said before, I try to be a logical person. If someone else sat down and told me this exact same story, I might think it sounded like a spy novel. I might think it was impossible, too. But the thing is, I am the one experiencing it. When you are the one sitting at the table, and you realize the two people placed next to you are tied perfectly back to a confidential trust fund you were just reviewing in your office a few hours ago... well, it does not feel impossible anymore. It feels incredibly, terrifyingly real. \n\nSo, yes, other people definitely consider it strange. And even when I sit there and force my logical brain to take over, even when I tell myself that maybe everyone else is right and it could just be one massive, unbelievable string of coincidences, I just cannot let it go. I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. So I do not know if that answers your question about things being outside the realm of what people expect, but that is the reality I am dealing with every single day, regardless of how impossible anyone else thinks it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your belief that these signals are being sent to you has ever changed, even slightly, when faced with clear evidence that they were not intended for you?\n\nPatient: I feel like we are going in circles a little bit here, doctor, because... well, honestly, no. No, it hasn't changed. Like I have been trying to explain since I first sat down in that chair, the whole reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know I keep bringing it back to that, but it is just so consuming. \n\nWhen someone puts what you're calling \"clear evidence\" right in front of my face—like, say, an email chain from an event coordinator showing how they randomized a guest list—maybe for a fraction of a second, my logical brain tries to grab onto it. I will literally stand in my kitchen, holding an invitation or a seating chart, and I will force myself to say out loud, \"This is just a coincidence. They didn't intend this for you.\" I really, truly try to talk myself down. But the relief, if you can even call it that, doesn't last. It never lasts. \n\nBecause then I start thinking about it, and I realize, well, of course they have an email chain that makes it look random. They are smart people, they aren't going to leave an obvious paper trail, right? And then the very next week, they are sending me little signals again through new invitations, or the specific panel topics at a symposium, or, again, exactly who sits near me at these events. It just keeps happening. So even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, and even when I look right at the supposed proof that it has nothing to do with me... I can’t really let it go. The underlying certainty never actually shifts. It is just this constant, heavy weight, and no amount of evidence they show me seems to actually turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were presented with clear, undeniable proof that these signals weren't actually directed at you, and if so, how long did it take before you started feeling certain again that they were?\n\nPatient: Look, doctor, I know we keep circling back to this same exact point, and I really don't mean to sound like a broken record, but I just... I need you to grasp the sheer scope of this, because it is exhausting. Like I've been saying, the entire reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. \n\nAnd when you ask about being presented with \"undeniable proof\" and how long it takes for me to feel certain again... it's practically instantaneous. It doesn't go away. Let me give you a very specific example so you understand what I'm dealing with. Just last month, I actually went up to one of the gala coordinators at a museum fundraiser. I was so worked up, I just casually asked her how they decided the seating for the evening, because I was placed right next to a prominent gallery director whose private trust accounts I had just finished auditing that very morning. \n\nAnd she literally pulled up her iPad right there in the foyer and showed me the event management software. She showed me how it auto-generates the seating arrangements based entirely on RSVP timestamps and whether people requested the vegetarian meal. It was right there in black and white. Data. Algorithms. And for a second—literally just a fraction of a second—looking at that screen, I told myself, 'See? It's just a computer program. It's random.' I really tried to lean into that.\n\nBut... how long did it take for the certainty to come back? Honestly, by the time I walked away from her and reached the coat check, the feeling was already rushing back over me. Maybe two minutes? Three at the most? Because as I was walking, I started thinking, well, who programmed the software? Who inputted the parameters? Who has access to the backend of the system? They are constantly sending me little signals through the invitations, the panel topics at our quarterly symposiums, even who sits near me at these events... and they are absolutely smart enough to make it look like a randomized computer output if anyone ever asks. \n\nSo, no matter what spreadsheet or software they show me, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The \"proof\" you're talking about just ends up feeling like another layer of the whole operation. I just can't shake it, no matter how hard I try to rationalize it away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where you were able to fully accept that one of these signals was truly random and not directed at you, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I mean... fully accept? Like, one hundred percent, without a single shadow of a doubt, just walk away and never think about it again? No. Honestly, doctor, no. \n\nI know we are kind of beating a dead horse here, and I know I keep circling back to the exact same explanation, but you have to understand that this is my entire life right now. It is exhausting to live with. I came in to see you specifically because, for a while now, I have been utterly convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And I know I keep saying that, but I need you to understand how all-encompassing it is when you are the one living through it. \n\nWhen they send me these little signals—whether it is the specific phrasing on the charity invitations they mail to my office, or the highly specific macroeconomic topics they suddenly decide to feature on a panel discussion, or, you know, exactly who ends up in the chair next to me at a networking dinner—I just can't turn a blind eye to it. I really try. I will sit in my car after one of these events, just gripping the steering wheel in the parking garage, and I will literally have a debate out loud with myself. I will say, 'Look, you are a rational guy, you work with numbers and portfolios, this could all just be one giant, ridiculous coincidence.' I try so hard to force myself to fully accept that it's random, just like you are asking me. I try to just breathe and let the logic take over.\n\nBut... no. I never fully accept it. Even when I sit there and tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The doubt always creeps right back in within minutes. It is like a splinter in my brain that I just can't reach. I start thinking about the statistical probability of all these things lining up perfectly with my confidential client list, and the rationalization just falls apart. So, I wish I could give you a nice, clean example of a time where I just brushed it off and moved on with my day, but that just hasn't happened. I just can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when, after being shown clear and direct evidence that one of these signals wasn't meant for you, you were able to completely stop believing in it, even for a short while—like a few minutes or an hour—before the feeling came back?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like you're looking for a specific window of time here, like you want me to say, 'Yes, I turned it off for forty-five minutes on a Tuesday,' but it just doesn't work like that. I know we are really drilling down into the exact same thing, and I appreciate your patience, but I just have to reiterate the sheer scale of why I'm sitting here in the first place. I came in because, for a while now, I’ve been entirely convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. That is the reality I am waking up to every single day, and it colors every single interaction I have. \n\nSo when you ask if I can just completely stop believing it for an hour... no. Absolutely not an hour. When they send me those little signals—and again, I'm talking about the very specific wording on charity invitations, the highly tailored panel topics at our symposiums, even exactly who sits near me at these networking events—when that happens, and someone shows me a piece of paper or a spreadsheet that supposedly proves it's all innocent... I might have a moment. Like I mentioned before with the iPad at the fundraiser. I might have sixty seconds where I am actively forcing my brain to hold onto that evidence. \n\nIt’s almost like... well, it's like holding your breath underwater, you know? I will literally stand there and tell myself, over and over, 'This could just be a coincidence. It's just a coincidence. Look at the evidence.' I try to just live in that logical space. \n\nBut the thing is, I'm just holding my breath. I haven't actually stopped believing it; I'm just suppressing it through sheer willpower for a minute or two. And then the second my guard drops, the second I stop actively repeating that mantra in my head, all the connections just flood right back in. I start thinking about the timing of my client meetings, the specific portfolios we discussed, and how it perfectly aligns with the event I'm at... and the belief is right back where it was, as heavy as ever. So, no, not an hour. Not even really a few minutes of genuine relief. Even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just... I can’t really let it go. The feeling never actually leaves me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a period lasting at least a month when you did not feel like these signals were being sent to you, even briefly?\n\nPatient: A month? Well, I mean... obviously, before all this started, my entire life was a period where I didn't feel like this. I was just doing my job, managing my clients' portfolios, going to museum fundraisers and networking dinners without a second thought. But if you're asking if I've had a month-long break from it since the signals actually started... no, that's impossible. \n\nBecause, doctor, you have to understand, this whole situation—this absolute, inescapable belief that people in the arts and finance circles are tracking my client meetings and sending me these signals—it has only really been going on for about two months in total. \n\nI know I probably talk about it like it's been my entire existence, and honestly, it feels like it's been years because of how mentally and physically draining it is to constantly be on high alert. But chronologically? It's been about two months. It started right around the time we were closing out the last fiscal quarter. That's when I first started noticing the patterns, you know? The highly specific wording on the charity invitations, the way certain trust managers and gallery directors were suddenly coordinating their moves and panel topics around my confidential auditing schedule. It all just clicked into place about eight weeks ago. \n\nSo, within that two-month window, there hasn't been any sort of break. Not a month, not a week, not even a quiet weekend. It’s been a constant, everyday reality for the last two months straight. Every single day I wake up and I have to navigate this elaborate web they've set up. So, no, there hasn't been a month where it just stopped or where I didn't feel it, because the entire experience, from the very first signal to me sitting right here in this chair talking to you, is only about two months old.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I mean, that is exactly what I have been trying to explain this entire time. I know I probably sound like a broken record, and I really do appreciate you bearing with me, but yes, that is precisely what happens. That is the exact crux of the issue. This is just a generic macroeconomic topic. I will actively tell myself it could just be a coincidence. I will know, logically, that it has to be random. 

But even with that knowledge sitting right there in my head... I still cannot stop believing it is part of this massive, deliberate pattern directed right at me. I just can't really let it go. It is like my brain acknowledges the coincidence, but my gut entirely rejects it. I will sit there, fully aware that a normal person would just shrug off the invitation or the seating arrangement, but I will still be completely paralyzed by the certainty that it is a signal. The logic just gets entirely swallowed up by the pattern, every single time.

Before this? No. Honestly, doctor, never. And I really need to emphasize that, because it’s important context for who I am and how my mind usually works. I mean, I have a bachelor's degree in finance, I work as a personal financial advisor... my entire life, my whole career, has been built on looking at hard data, assessing risk, and being completely objective. I've never been the type of person to just invent things or cling to some strange idea that everyone else said was false. I was always the most level-headed, rational guy in the room. If someone said, 'Hey, that's not true,' and showed me the proof, I'd just say, 'Okay, you're right,' and move on. 

That is exactly why this current situation is so incredibly jarring for me, and why I came in to see you in the first place. Because I don't have a history of this kind of thinking. It’s just that, for a while now, I’ve been utterly convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know we keep circling back to this, but I just can't overstate how much it has derailed my normal way of functioning. 

They are sending me these little signals through the charity invitations, the specific panel topics at symposiums, even exactly who sits near me at these networking events. And the worst part—the part that really gets to me, considering how logical and grounded I've always been my entire life—is that even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The feeling just stays there, overriding all my common sense. So, no, before these last couple of months, I've never experienced anything even remotely like this. I've always trusted my own reality until now.

It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I haven't lost interest in my work—in fact, I'm probably more focused on it now than ever, given everything that's going on with the networking events and trying to figure out who's connected to who. I'm not staying up all night painting or coming up with wild business schemes or talking a mile a minute. I mean, sure, I'm talking a lot right now, but that's just because I'm trying to explain all this to you so you understand exactly what's happening. But in my day-to-day life, I'm pretty measured. I have to be. You can't be bouncing off the walls when you're advising someone on a million-dollar trust fund. So, no, I haven't had any big swings like that, high or low. It's just this constant, steady pressure of dealing with the signals and the subtext, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there have been times when you were absolutely sure about something that turned out not to be true, even when others showed you evidence that it wasn’t accurate?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s exactly why I came in to talk to someone in the first place. Because the whole \"turned out not to be true\" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting. To me, it feels incredibly, undeniably real. \n\nFor a while now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain people—specifically this sort of overlapping group in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati—are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And they aren't just tracking it, they’re going out of their way to send me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It plays out in these hyper-specific ways, you know? Like through the invitations I receive in the mail, or the specific panel topics at these networking events, or even exactly who sits near me at a charity dinner. I’ll sit down at a table of ten, and the two people placed immediately to my left and right will be directly connected to a private trust I was just reviewing in my office that very morning. \n\nAnd, you know, people will tell me I'm overthinking it. A colleague or a friend will say, \"Oh, the event planners just seat people by donor bracket, it's totally random, look at the spreadsheet.\" And I hear them, I really do. I try to be a logical person—I have to be, with my degree and my job. I sit there and I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, that maybe the panel topic was planned six months ago and it's just a fluke that it perfectly mirrors my current client roster. But even when they show me that supposed evidence, or explain the logistics of the event, I just... I can’t really let it go. I can't shake the absolute certainty that it's intentional, that they are watching my moves. Because how many coincidences can you have before it's a deliberate pattern, right? So I guess that’s the thing I'm so sure about, even when everyone else is trying to prove to me that it's just in my head.\n\nDoctor: When people have explained to you that the events you notice could be coincidences or planned in advance, do you accept that explanation, or do you still feel certain that the events are specifically directed at you?\n\nPatient: Well, right, like I was just trying to explain a second ago, I really, really try to accept those explanations. I do. I sit there and I listen to my colleague tell me that a panel topic was booked six months in advance, or I look at the event planner's seating chart, and my logical brain—the part of me that got my bachelor's degree and analyzes complex financial data all day—tries to say, \"Okay, yes, that makes sense on paper.\" But... no. Ultimately, no, I don't accept it. I can't. \n\nBecause the moment I step back into that room, or the moment I open another invitation in the mail, I get this overwhelming, undeniable certainty that it is absolutely, specifically directed at me. The arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati, they are incredibly tight-knit, right? Everyone knows everyone, money moves between the same few institutions and galleries. And I am just completely convinced, down to my bones, that these specific people in those circles are quietly tracking exactly which clients I'm meeting with behind closed doors. \n\nIt's not just a passing thought that I can brush off. It's this heavy, constant awareness. I'll be sitting there at a charity dinner, looking at the two people seated next to me, realizing they tie perfectly back to a portfolio I was just reviewing, and I'll tell myself, \"It's just a coincidence, it's just random chance.\" I repeat it in my head. But I can't let it go. I just can't. Because how can it be random when the signals are just so incredibly precise? The panel topics, the invitations, the seating... it all perfectly aligns with my private client meetings. So, even when people try to explain it away with logistics or spreadsheets, it doesn't change what I know I'm experiencing. I hear their explanations, but deep down, I am absolutely certain they are tracking me and sending me these little signals. I just wish I knew exactly what their end game was, you know? I cannot. Because it is happening to me, and the signals are just too precise. So I do not know if that answers your question about things being outside the realm of what people expect, but that is the reality I am dealing with every single day, regardless of how impossible anyone else thinks it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if your belief that these signals are being sent to you has ever changed, even slightly, when faced with clear evidence that they were not intended for you?\n\nPatient: I feel like we are going in circles a little bit here, doctor, because... well, honestly, no. No, it hasn't changed. Like I have been trying to explain since I first sat down in that chair, the whole reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know I keep bringing it back to that, but it is just so consuming. \n\nWhen someone puts what you're calling \"clear evidence\" right in front of my face—like, say, an email chain from an event coordinator showing how they randomized a guest list—maybe for a fraction of a second, my logical brain tries to grab onto it. I will literally stand in my kitchen, holding an invitation or a seating chart, and I will force myself to say out loud, \"This is just a coincidence. They didn't intend this for you.\" I really, truly try to talk myself down. But the relief, if you can even call it that, doesn't last. It never lasts. \n\nBecause then I start thinking about it, and I realize, well, of course they have an email chain that makes it look random. They are smart people, they aren't going to leave an obvious paper trail, right? And then the very next week, they are sending me little signals again through new invitations, or the specific panel topics at a symposium, or, again, exactly who sits near me at these events. It just keeps happening. So even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, and even when I look right at the supposed proof that it has nothing to do with me... I can’t really let it go. The underlying certainty never actually shifts. It is just this constant, heavy weight, and no amount of evidence they show me seems to actually turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were presented with clear, undeniable proof that these signals weren't actually directed at you, and if so, how long did it take before you started feeling certain again that they were?\n\nPatient: Look, doctor, I know we keep circling back to this same exact point, and I really don't mean to sound like a broken record, but I just... I need you to grasp the sheer scope of this, because it is exhausting. Like I've been saying, the entire reason I came in is because for a while now I’ve been convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. \n\nAnd when you ask about being presented with \"undeniable proof\" and how long it takes for me to feel certain again... it's practically instantaneous. It doesn't go away. Let me give you a very specific example so you understand what I'm dealing with. Just last month, I actually went up to one of the gala coordinators at a museum fundraiser. I was so worked up, I just casually asked her how they decided the seating for the evening, because I was placed right next to a prominent gallery director whose private trust accounts I had just finished auditing that very morning. \n\nAnd she literally pulled up her iPad right there in the foyer and showed me the event management software. She showed me how it auto-generates the seating arrangements based entirely on RSVP timestamps and whether people requested the vegetarian meal. It was right there in black and white. Data. Algorithms. And for a second—literally just a fraction of a second—looking at that screen, I told myself, 'See? It's just a computer program. It's random.' I really tried to lean into that.\n\nBut... how long did it take for the certainty to come back? Honestly, by the time I walked away from her and reached the coat check, the feeling was already rushing back over me. Maybe two minutes? Three at the most? Because as I was walking, I started thinking, well, who programmed the software? Who inputted the parameters? Who has access to the backend of the system? They are constantly sending me little signals through the invitations, the panel topics at our quarterly symposiums, even who sits near me at these events... and they are absolutely smart enough to make it look like a randomized computer output if anyone ever asks. \n\nSo, no matter what spreadsheet or software they show me, even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The \"proof\" you're talking about just ends up feeling like another layer of the whole operation. I just can't shake it, no matter how hard I try to rationalize it away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation where you were able to fully accept that one of these signals was truly random and not directed at you, even briefly?\n\nPatient: I mean... fully accept? Like, one hundred percent, without a single shadow of a doubt, just walk away and never think about it again? No. Honestly, doctor, no. \n\nI know we are kind of beating a dead horse here, and I know I keep circling back to the exact same explanation, but you have to understand that this is my entire life right now. It is exhausting to live with. I came in to see you specifically because, for a while now, I have been utterly convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking exactly which clients I meet with. And I know I keep saying that, but I need you to understand how all-encompassing it is when you are the one living through it. \n\nWhen they send me these little signals—whether it is the specific phrasing on the charity invitations they mail to my office, or the highly specific macroeconomic topics they suddenly decide to feature on a panel discussion, or, you know, exactly who ends up in the chair next to me at a networking dinner—I just can't turn a blind eye to it. I really try. I will sit in my car after one of these events, just gripping the steering wheel in the parking garage, and I will literally have a debate out loud with myself. I will say, 'Look, you are a rational guy, you work with numbers and portfolios, this could all just be one giant, ridiculous coincidence.' I try so hard to force myself to fully accept that it's random, just like you are asking me. I try to just breathe and let the logic take over.\n\nBut... no. I never fully accept it. Even when I sit there and tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The doubt always creeps right back in within minutes. It is like a splinter in my brain that I just can't reach. I start thinking about the statistical probability of all these things lining up perfectly with my confidential client list, and the rationalization just falls apart. So, I wish I could give you a nice, clean example of a time where I just brushed it off and moved on with my day, but that just hasn't happened. I just can't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when, after being shown clear and direct evidence that one of these signals wasn't meant for you, you were able to completely stop believing in it, even for a short while—like a few minutes or an hour—before the feeling came back?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like you're looking for a specific window of time here, like you want me to say, 'Yes, I turned it off for forty-five minutes on a Tuesday,' but it just doesn't work like that. I know we are really drilling down into the exact same thing, and I appreciate your patience, but I just have to reiterate the sheer scale of why I'm sitting here in the first place. I came in because, for a while now, I’ve been entirely convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. That is the reality I am waking up to every single day, and it colors every single interaction I have. \n\nSo when you ask if I can just completely stop believing it for an hour... no. Absolutely not an hour. When they send me those little signals—and again, I'm talking about the very specific wording on charity invitations, the highly tailored panel topics at our symposiums, even exactly who sits near me at these networking events—when that happens, and someone shows me a piece of paper or a spreadsheet that supposedly proves it's all innocent... I might have a moment. Like I mentioned before with the iPad at the fundraiser. I might have sixty seconds where I am actively forcing my brain to hold onto that evidence. \n\nIt’s almost like... well, it's like holding your breath underwater, you know? I will literally stand there and tell myself, over and over, 'This could just be a coincidence. It's just a coincidence. Look at the evidence.' I try to just live in that logical space. \n\nBut the thing is, I'm just holding my breath. I haven't actually stopped believing it; I'm just suppressing it through sheer willpower for a minute or two. And then the second my guard drops, the second I stop actively repeating that mantra in my head, all the connections just flood right back in. I start thinking about the timing of my client meetings, the specific portfolios we discussed, and how it perfectly aligns with the event I'm at... and the belief is right back where it was, as heavy as ever. So, no, not an hour. Not even really a few minutes of genuine relief. Even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I just... I can’t really let it go. The feeling never actually leaves me.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a period lasting at least a month when you did not feel like these signals were being sent to you, even briefly?\n\nPatient: A month? Well, I mean... obviously, before all this started, my entire life was a period where I didn't feel like this. I was just doing my job, managing my clients' portfolios, going to museum fundraisers and networking dinners without a second thought. But if you're asking if I've had a month-long break from it since the signals actually started... no, that's impossible. \n\nBecause, doctor, you have to understand, this whole situation—this absolute, inescapable belief that people in the arts and finance circles are tracking my client meetings and sending me these signals—it has only really been going on for about two months in total. \n\nI know I probably talk about it like it's been my entire existence, and honestly, it feels like it's been years because of how mentally and physically draining it is to constantly be on high alert. But chronologically? It's been about two months. It started right around the time we were closing out the last fiscal quarter. That's when I first started noticing the patterns, you know? The highly specific wording on the charity invitations, the way certain trust managers and gallery directors were suddenly coordinating their moves and panel topics around my confidential auditing schedule. It all just clicked into place about eight weeks ago. \n\nSo, within that two-month window, there hasn't been any sort of break. Not a month, not a week, not even a quiet weekend. It’s been a constant, everyday reality for the last two months straight. Every single day I wake up and I have to navigate this elaborate web they've set up. So, no, there hasn't been a month where it just stopped or where I didn't feel it, because the entire experience, from the very first signal to me sitting right here in this chair talking to you, is only about two months old.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when, despite knowing that a signal you noticed was clearly random or coincidental, you were still unable to stop believing it was part of a larger pattern directed at you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I mean, that is exactly what I have been trying to explain this entire time. I know I probably sound like a broken record, and I really do appreciate you bearing with me, but yes, that is precisely what happens. That is the exact crux of the issue. Like I mentioned, the whole reason I even made the appointment to come see you is because, for a while now, I have been just completely convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. \n\nAnd to your point about knowing it is random... yes. I will look at the signals they send, the specific wording on those charity invitations, the highly tailored panel topics at the symposiums, or even just who happens to sit near me at a networking event, and my logical brain will kick in. The part of me with a degree in finance, the guy who analyzes market trends for a living, will look at it and say, okay, statistically, this is just a standard seating chart. This is just a generic macroeconomic topic. I will actively tell myself it could just be a coincidence. I will know, logically, that it has to be random. \n\nBut even with that knowledge sitting right there in my head... I still cannot stop believing it is part of this massive, deliberate pattern directed right at me. I just can't really let it go. It is like my brain acknowledges the coincidence, but my gut entirely rejects it. I will sit there, fully aware that a normal person would just shrug off the invitation or the seating arrangement, but I will still be completely paralyzed by the certainty that it is a signal. The logic just gets entirely swallowed up by the pattern, every single time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life, even before these signals started, where you strongly believed something that others said wasn’t true, even if it wasn’t related to being watched or followed?\n\nPatient: Before this? No. Honestly, doctor, never. And I really need to emphasize that, because it’s important context for who I am and how my mind usually works. I mean, I have a bachelor's degree in finance, I work as a personal financial advisor... my entire life, my whole career, has been built on looking at hard data, assessing risk, and being completely objective. I've never been the type of person to just invent things or cling to some strange idea that everyone else said was false. I was always the most level-headed, rational guy in the room. If someone said, 'Hey, that's not true,' and showed me the proof, I'd just say, 'Okay, you're right,' and move on. \n\nThat is exactly why this current situation is so incredibly jarring for me, and why I came in to see you in the first place. Because I don't have a history of this kind of thinking. It’s just that, for a while now, I’ve been utterly convinced that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. And I know we keep circling back to this, but I just can't overstate how much it has derailed my normal way of functioning. \n\nThey are sending me these little signals through the charity invitations, the specific panel topics at symposiums, even exactly who sits near me at these networking events. And the worst part—the part that really gets to me, considering how logical and grounded I've always been my entire life—is that even when I tell myself it could just be a coincidence, I can’t really let it go. The feeling just stays there, overriding all my common sense. So, no, before these last couple of months, I've never experienced anything even remotely like this. I've always trusted my own reality until now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it’s... it’s a bit complicated, because, like I’ve been saying, my logical brain is almost always running in the background. That’s just the finance training kicking in, right? So, to say I’m completely unable to even consider the possibility that it's all random... I don't think that's entirely accurate. Because, as I mentioned, I do tell myself it could just be a coincidence. I really try to. I’ll be holding one of those invitations, or looking at the schedule for a symposium, and I will force myself to think, 'Okay, maybe this has absolutely nothing to do with me.' 

But the core issue—and again, the whole reason I came in here—is that for a while now, I’ve been so deeply convinced that these certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are quietly tracking which clients I meet with. So, even when I have that brief, fleeting second of logical thought, even when I explicitly tell myself it could just be a coincidence... I can’t really let it go. It just doesn't stick. The logic evaporates almost immediately. 

I’ll try to push it out of my head, I really will, but the sheer weight of those little signals they keep sending—the specific panel topics, the exact people they seat near me at events—it just completely crushes whatever rational thought I managed to scrape together. I can maybe consider the possibility that it's not real for a fraction of a second, but then the absolute certainty just comes rushing right back in and takes over. It’s honestly exhausting. You'd think that being able to recognize a coincidence would be enough to break it, but it’s just not. I can't let it go, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I, uh, I appreciate you seeing me. I’m not entirely sure where to begin, honestly, because it’s a lot to unpack, and it’s been going on for a while now. I work as a personal financial advisor, you see, mostly dealing with clients in the arts and finance sectors here in Cincinnati. It’s a tight-knit community, very interconnected, lots of networking events, gallery openings, charity dinners, that sort of thing. But lately, well, for a good stretch of time now, I’ve been absolutely convinced that certain individuals in these circles are... tracking me. Not in an overt way, but quietly monitoring exactly which clients I’m meeting with, who I’m bringing on board, my entire portfolio. And it’s not just a passing thought; they’re sending me these little signals to let me know they’re watching. Like, I’ll get an invitation to a very specific event right after a meeting with a new client, or the topics at a panel discussion will perfectly mirror a private conversation I just had. Even the seating arrangements at these dinners—who they place next to me—it feels incredibly deliberate, like a message. And I know, I know how it sounds, and there are times I try to tell myself, \"Come on, it’s just a coincidence, it’s a small town,\" but... I just can’t let it go. It’s always there in the back of my mind, this constant feeling of being watched and signaled to, and it’s really starting to weigh on me, which is why I finally decided I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or sensed anything that others around you don’t seem to notice, like voices, images, or other sensations that might be part of these experiences you're describing?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not exactly. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's not that I heard a voice telling me they were watching, it's just that the connection was so undeniable. So, no, I don't see or hear things that aren't physically present. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. But if I really sit down and trace it back to when it became this constant, undeniable presence in my life... I'd say it's been about two solid months. \n\nYeah, two months. Because I remember it was right around the time I brought on this new client—a sculptor who just got a major grant—and suddenly the networking events started feeling different. The invitations changed. And since then, it hasn't really stopped. There hasn't been a week or even a few days where I could just go to a dinner or a meeting and just be a financial advisor. It's always there. Every single day, I'm analyzing the seating charts, I'm looking at who is RSVPing to what, I'm trying to figure out why a certain gallery owner is suddenly so interested in my portfolio. It's just been this continuous, unbroken stretch for about eight weeks now where I can't shake the feeling that they're tracking exactly who I'm meeting with and sending me these little signals to let me know they're doing it. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. I mean, like I was saying before, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there. That's never happened to me, not even for a short time, let alone a whole month. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. It's not some delusion or something my mind is just making up out of thin air. \n\nI've never had a period where I was completely out of touch with reality, if that's what you're getting at. I've always been very grounded, very detail-oriented—you have to be in my line of work, right? Managing people's money, understanding the nuances of the market, you can't afford to be living in a fantasy world. So no, I haven't had any months where I was hallucinating or believing in things that were demonstrably false. I mean, it's not like I'm hearing someone whispering in my ear when I'm alone in my apartment, or seeing people who aren't there, or, you know, feeling things on my skin. It's nothing like a hallucination, if that's what you're asking. It's more about the... the reality of the situations I'm in. The people are real, the events are real, the conversations are happening. It's just the *meaning* behind them that feels so heavy and deliberate. \n\nLike, for instance, last Tuesday, I was at this gallery opening downtown—a client of mine was showing some new pieces—and the ambient noise, the chatter, it was just normal crowd noise, right? But it was the *way* two people across the room looked at me right as the curator mentioned a specific financial term during his speech. It's more that I'm picking up on the subtext, the hidden layers of what's actually happening right in front of everyone, even if they're completely oblivious to it. It's like everyone else is watching a movie, but I'm the only one reading the subtitles, you know? And it's exhausting, having to constantly analyze every interaction, every glance, every invitation, trying to decode what they're really trying to say to me without actually saying it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, has this feeling of being watched and receiving hidden messages been present for most of the time, without significant breaks?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the exact timeline, because it didn't just start overnight, you know? It wasn't like I woke up one morning and suddenly everyone was sending me messages. It sort of crept up on me. It's exhausting, honestly, because my brain just never gets to turn off and take a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you experienced things like hearing voices or seeing things that others didn't, or felt completely certain about something that just wasn't true, even for a short while, that lasted more than a month?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not like that. And as for believing things that aren't true... well, that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because I *know* what I'm experiencing is real. The events, the people, the signals—they're all actually happening. What I'm dealing with now, this feeling of being monitored and signaled to by the people in my professional circle, it's the only time I've ever experienced anything like this. And even then, it's rooted in actual events, just... interpreted differently, I guess. But no, nothing like what you're describing, not in my whole life. I've always been very level-headed, which is why all of this is so incredibly frustrating and confusing for me to navigate.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time when you felt unusually down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, or times when you felt unusually energized, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really either of those extremes. I mean, I haven't been depressed, if that's what you mean by feeling down or hopeless. I still get up every day, I still go to the office, I still manage my clients' portfolios. I haven't lost interest in my work—in fact, I'm probably more focused on it now than ever, given everything that's going on with the networking events and trying to figure out who's connected to who. Because the whole \"turned out not to be true\" part is where I’m really getting stuck, and it's what makes this all so exhausting.     "duration_verified": "Symptoms present for approximately two months, with no remission during that time",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "Delusions present for at least one month",
      "Absence of prominent hallucinations",
      "Absence of mood episodes or manic symptoms",
      "No history of similar beliefs prior to onset"
    ]
  }
